# Research: subset-b-000583

Grouped research for ceph-client Devicetree binding YAML schemas covering IOMMU, IPMI, LED, backlight, IR LED, and common LED bindings. Each section preserves the source path and is wrapped for deterministic reconciliation into source-tree-aligned per-file reports.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/iommu/arm,smmu.yaml

**Purpose:** Defines the Linux Devicetree JSON-schema contract for `ARM System MMU Architecture Implementation`. In this source tree it is a IOMMU provider or IOMMU-adjacent binding; it constrains board DTS/DTB nodes so kernel OF matching and subsystem code can discover the hardware resources consistently. This is declarative ABI documentation, not executable Ceph client code, but it is part of the imported kernel documentation corpus that protects hardware-description compatibility. The source description says: ARM SoCs may contain an implementation of the ARM System Memory Management Unit Architecture, which can be used to provide 1 or 2 stages of address translation to bus masters external to the CPU. The SMMU may also raise interrupts in response to various fault conditions.

**Important APIs/types/functions:** The public surface is the schema itself: `$id` `http://devicetree.org/schemas/iommu/arm,smmu.yaml#`, `$schema` `http://devicetree.org/meta-schemas/core.yaml#`, `title`, `maintainers`, `properties`, `required`, composition keywords, closure rules, and `examples` consumed by `dt-schema`. Compatible strings are `qcom,msm8996-smmu-v2`, `qcom,msm8998-smmu-v2`, `qcom,sdm630-smmu-v2`, `qcom,sm6375-smmu-v2`, `qcom,smmu-v2`, `qcom,eliza-smmu-500`, `qcom,glymur-smmu-500`, `qcom,kaanapali-smmu-500`, `qcom,milos-smmu-500`, `qcom,qcm2290-smmu-500`, `qcom,qcs615-smmu-500`, `qcom,qcs8300-smmu-500`, `qcom,qdu1000-smmu-500`, `qcom,sa8255p-smmu-500`, `qcom,sa8775p-smmu-500`, `qcom,sar2130p-smmu-500`, `qcom,sc7180-smmu-500`, `qcom,sc7280-smmu-500`, and 38 more. Required properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `#global-interrupts`, `#iommu-cells`, `interrupts`. Important top-level properties include `$nodename`, `compatible`, `reg`, `#global-interrupts`, `#iommu-cells`, `interrupts`, `dma-coherent`, `calxeda,smmu-secure-config-access`, `stream-match-mask`, `clock-names`, `clocks`, `power-domains`, `nvidia,memory-controller`. Patterned or child-node surfaces include none declared. Direct schema references are `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/phandle`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32`. Maintainers: Will Deacon <will@kernel.org>, Robin Murphy <Robin.Murphy@arm.com>. Top-level property policy is `additionalProperties: false`.

**Control flow:** Runtime control flow is supplied by Devicetree tooling and kernel drivers rather than by this YAML. `dt_binding_check` loads the core meta-schema, resolves `$ref` dependencies, applies constants/enums/cardinality/range checks, evaluates composition and conditional branches (`oneOf`, `allOf`, `if`, `then`, `else`, `anyOf`), and validates the embedded examples. At boot, the compiled DTB node is matched by `compatible`; the relevant IOMMU driver or common subsystem reads resource properties, phandles, child nodes, and cells before registering devices or provider relationships. Child/provider surfaces to watch here are none declared.

**State and persistence behavior:** The file owns no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data. Its persistence is ABI persistence: accepted DTS nodes encode hardware identity, register windows, interrupts, power/clock/reset topology, phandle cell widths, channel indexes, and child LED/IOMMU/IPMI layout for long-lived boards. Tightening `required`, changing compatible fallback order, altering cell counts, or changing closure policy can reject existing DTBs or make drivers parse resources differently.

**Dependencies and integration points:** Integrated with IOMMU stream IDs, DMA translation, device isolation, and `iommus`/`#iommu-cells` style phandle integration. Schema dependencies include `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/phandle`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32`; examples may also include dt-bindings headers. Resource integration is through properties such as `compatible` for driver match key and hardware ABI identifier; `reg` for MMIO, I2C/SPI address, or child register address; `interrupts` for interrupt routing; `clocks` for clock provider phandles; `clock-names` for named clock inputs; `power-domains` for genpd power-domain attachment; `#iommu-cells` for IOMMU client specifier cell count. Driver integration is through `compatible`, while subsystem integration is through phandle arrays, provider cell counts, common schemas, and child-node schemas inherited or declared here.

**Risks and edge cases:** Main risks are schema ABI drift, missing or stale compatible coverage, incorrect min/max item constraints, wrong `#*-cells` values, resource names that diverge from driver expectations, conditionals that validate one SoC or chip variant while rejecting another valid one, and examples that no longer reflect real DTS users. Closure policy (`additionalProperties: false`) is useful for catching misspellings but can also block legitimate extensions. For LED/backlight children, channel numbering and `reg`/`led-sources` constraints are common failure points; for IOMMU bindings, stream IDs and cell widths are especially sensitive; for IPMI bindings, host/BMC address and register-resource shape must match the driver.

**Test signals:** Run `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/iommu/arm,smmu.yaml` and `make dtbs_check` against DTS files using every compatible listed here. The 2 embedded example block(s) should compile through `dtc` and validate against referenced schemas. Useful negative tests remove each required property, add an unknown property under the declared closure policy, exercise compatible-specific conditionals, and verify the matching Linux driver still probes with the resources, child nodes, and phandle specifiers described by this binding.
<!-- END_FILE_RESEARCH: sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/iommu/arm,smmu.yaml -->

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/iommu/mediatek,iommu.yaml

**Purpose:** Defines the Linux Devicetree JSON-schema contract for `MediaTek IOMMU Architecture Implementation`. In this source tree it is a IOMMU provider or IOMMU-adjacent binding; it constrains board DTS/DTB nodes so kernel OF matching and subsystem code can discover the hardware resources consistently. This is declarative ABI documentation, not executable Ceph client code, but it is part of the imported kernel documentation corpus that protects hardware-description compatibility. The source description says: Some MediaTek SOCs contain a Multimedia Memory Management Unit (M4U), and this M4U have two generations of HW architecture. Generation one uses flat pagetable, and only supports 4K size page mapping. Generation two uses the ARM Short-Descriptor translation table format for address translation. About the M4U Hardware Block Diagram, please check below: EMI (External Memory Interface) | m4u (Multimedia Memory Management Unit) | +--------+ | | gals0-rx gals1-rx (Global Async Local Sync rx) | | | | gals0-tx gals1-tx (Global Async Local Sync tx) | | Some SoCs may have GALS. +--------+ | SMI Common(Smart Multimedia Interface Common) | +----------------+------- | | | gals-rx There may be GALS in some larbs. | | | | | gals-tx | | SMI larb0 SMI larb1 ... SoCs have several SMI local arbiter(larb). (display) (vdec) | | | | +-----+-----+ +----+----+ | | | | | | | | |... | | | ... There are different ports in each larb. | | | | | | OVL0 RDMA0 WDMA0 MC PP VLD As above, The Multimedia HW will go through SMI and M4U while it access EMI. SMI is a bridge between m4u and the Multimedia HW. It contain smi local arbiter and smi common. It will control whether the Multimedia HW should go through the m4u for translation or bypass it and talk directly with EMI. And also SMI help control the power domain and clocks for each local arbiter. Normally we specify a local arbiter(larb) for each multimedia HW like display, video decode, and camera. And there are different ports in each larb. Take a example, There are many ports like MC, PP, VLD in the video decode local arbiter, all these ports are according to the video HW. In some SoCs, there may be a GALS(Global Async Local Sync) module between smi-common and m4u, and additional GALS module between smi-larb and smi-common. GALS can been seen as a "asynchronous fifo" which could help synchronize for the modules in different clock frequency.

**Important APIs/types/functions:** The public surface is the schema itself: `$id` `http://devicetree.org/schemas/iommu/mediatek,iommu.yaml#`, `$schema` `http://devicetree.org/meta-schemas/core.yaml#`, `title`, `maintainers`, `properties`, `required`, composition keywords, closure rules, and `examples` consumed by `dt-schema`. # sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/iommu/qcom,tbu.yaml

**Purpose:** Defines the Linux Devicetree JSON-schema contract for `Qualcomm TBU (Translation Buffer Unit)`. In this source tree it is a IOMMU provider or IOMMU-adjacent binding; it constrains board DTS/DTB nodes so kernel OF matching and subsystem code can discover the hardware resources consistently. This is declarative ABI documentation, not executable Ceph client code, but it is part of the imported kernel documentation corpus that protects hardware-description compatibility. The source description says: The Qualcomm SMMU500 implementation consists of TCU and TBU. The TBU contains a Translation Lookaside Buffer (TLB) that caches page tables. TBUs provides debug features to trace and trigger debug transactions. There are multiple TBU instances with each client core.

**Important APIs/types/functions:** The public surface is the schema itself: `$id` `http://devicetree.org/schemas/iommu/qcom,tbu.yaml#`, `$schema` `http://devicetree.org/meta-schemas/core.yaml#`, `title`, `maintainers`, `properties`, `required`, composition keywords, closure rules, and `examples` consumed by `dt-schema`. Direct schema references are `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/phandle-array`. Maintainers: Georgi Djakov <quic_c_gdjako@quicinc.com>. Top-level property policy is `additionalProperties: false`.

For LED/backlight children, channel numbering and `reg`/`led-sources` constraints are common failure points; for IOMMU bindings, stream IDs and cell widths are especially sensitive; for IPMI bindings, host/BMC address and register-resource shape must match the driver.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/iommu/riscv,iommu.yaml

**Purpose:** Defines the Linux Devicetree JSON-schema contract for `RISC-V IOMMU Architecture Implementation`. In this source tree it is a IOMMU provider or IOMMU-adjacent binding; it constrains board DTS/DTB nodes so kernel OF matching and subsystem code can discover the hardware resources consistently. This is declarative ABI documentation, not executable Ceph client code, but it is part of the imported kernel documentation corpus that protects hardware-description compatibility. The source description says: The RISC-V IOMMU provides memory address translation and isolation for input and output devices, supporting per-device translation context, shared process address spaces including the ATS and PRI components of the PCIe specification, two stage address translation and MSI remapping. It supports identical translation table format to the RISC-V address translation tables with page level access and protection attributes. Hardware uses in-memory command and fault reporting queues with wired interrupt or MSI notifications. Visit https://github.com/riscv-non-isa/riscv-iommu for more details. For information on assigning RISC-V IOMMU to its peripheral devices, see generic IOMMU bindings.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/iommu/samsung,sysmmu.yaml

**Purpose:** Defines the Linux Devicetree JSON-schema contract for `Samsung Exynos IOMMU H/W, System MMU (System Memory Management Unit)`. In this source tree it is a IOMMU provider or IOMMU-adjacent binding; it constrains board DTS/DTB nodes so kernel OF matching and subsystem code can discover the hardware resources consistently. This is declarative ABI documentation, not executable Ceph client code, but it is part of the imported kernel documentation corpus that protects hardware-description compatibility. The source description says: Samsung's Exynos architecture contains System MMUs that enables scattered physical memory chunks visible as a contiguous region to DMA-capable peripheral devices like MFC, FIMC, FIMD, GScaler, FIMC-IS and so forth. System MMU is an IOMMU and supports identical translation table format to ARMv7 translation tables with minimum set of page properties including access permissions, shareability and security protection. In addition, System MMU has another capabilities like L2 TLB or block-fetch buffers to minimize translation latency. System MMUs are in many to one relation with peripheral devices, i.e. single peripheral device might have multiple System MMUs (usually one for each bus master), but one System MMU can handle transactions from only one peripheral device. The relation between a System MMU and the peripheral device needs to be defined in device node of the peripheral device. MFC in all Exynos SoCs and FIMD, M2M Scalers and G2D in Exynos5420 has 2 System MMUs. * MFC has one System MMU on its left and right bus. * FIMD in Exynos5420 has one System MMU for window 0 and 4, the other system MMU for window 1, 2 and 3. * M2M Scalers and G2D in Exynos5420 has one System MMU on the read channel and the other System MMU on the write channel. For information on assigning System MMU controller to its peripheral devices, see generic IOMMU bindings.

**Important APIs/types/functions:** The public surface is the schema itself: `$id` `http://devicetree.org/schemas/iommu/samsung,sysmmu.yaml#`, `$schema` `http://devicetree.org/meta-schemas/core.yaml#`, `title`, `maintainers`, `properties`, `required`, composition keywords, closure rules, and `examples` consumed by `dt-schema`. # sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/ipmi/ipmb-dev.yaml

**Purpose:** Defines the Linux Devicetree JSON-schema contract for `The Intelligent Platform Management Bus(IPMB) Device`. In this source tree it is a IPMI/BMC management interface binding; it constrains board DTS/DTB nodes so kernel OF matching and subsystem code can discover the hardware resources consistently. This is declarative ABI documentation, not executable Ceph client code, but it is part of the imported kernel documentation corpus that protects hardware-description compatibility. The source description says: The IPMB is an I2C bus which provides interconnection between a Baseboard Management Controller(BMC) and chassis electronics. The BMC sends IPMI requests to intelligent controllers like Satellite Management Controller(MC) devices via IPMB and the device sends responses back to the BMC. This device uses an I2C slave device to send and receive IPMB messages, either on a BMC or other MC. A miscellaneous device provices a user space program to communicate with the kernel and the backend device. Some IPMB devices only support the I2C protocol and not the SMB protocol. IPMB communications protocol Specification V1.0 https://www.intel.com/content/dam/www/public/us/en/documents/product-briefs/ipmp-spec-v1.0.pdf

This is declarative ABI documentation, not executable Ceph client code, but it is part of the imported kernel documentation corpus that protects hardware-description compatibility.

**Important APIs/types/functions:** The public surface is the schema itself: `$id` `http://devicetree.org/schemas/leds/backlight/pwm-backlight.yaml#`, `$schema` `http://devicetree.org/meta-schemas/core.yaml#`, `title`, `maintainers`, `properties`, `required`, composition keywords, closure rules, and `examples` consumed by `dt-schema`. Compatible strings are `pwm-backlight`. Required properties are `compatible`, `pwms`. Important top-level properties include `compatible`, `pwms`, `pwm-names`, `power-supply`, `enable-gpios`, `post-pwm-on-delay-ms`, `pwm-off-delay-ms`, `num-interpolated-steps`. Patterned or child-node surfaces include none declared. Direct schema references are `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32`, `common.yaml#`. Maintainers: Lee Jones <lee@kernel.org>, Daniel Thompson <daniel.thompson@linaro.org>, Jingoo Han <jingoohan1@gmail.com>. Top-level property policy is `unevaluatedProperties: false`.

**Control flow:** Runtime control flow is supplied by Devicetree tooling and kernel drivers rather than by this YAML. `dt_binding_check` loads the core meta-schema, resolves `$ref` dependencies, applies constants/enums/cardinality/range checks, evaluates composition and conditional branches (`allOf`), and validates the embedded examples. At boot, the compiled DTB node is matched by `compatible`; the relevant LED backlight driver or common subsystem reads resource properties, phandles, child nodes, and cells before registering devices or provider relationships. Child/provider surfaces to watch here are `power-supply`.

**State and persistence behavior:** The file owns no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data. Its persistence is ABI persistence: accepted DTS nodes encode hardware identity, register windows, interrupts, power/clock/reset topology, phandle cell widths, channel indexes, and child LED/IOMMU/IPMI layout for long-lived boards. Tightening `required`, changing compatible fallback order, altering cell counts, or changing closure policy can reject existing DTBs or make drivers parse resources differently.

**Dependencies and integration points:** Integrated with backlight class devices, LED current/PWM/enable controls, regulator/GPIO resources, and brightness policy. Schema dependencies include `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32`, `common.yaml#`; examples may also include dt-bindings headers. Resource integration is through properties such as `compatible` for driver match key and hardware ABI identifier; `pwms` for PWM backlight or LED control input; `enable-gpios` for enable GPIO control. Driver integration is through `compatible`, while subsystem integration is through phandle arrays, provider cell counts, common schemas, and child-node schemas inherited or declared here.

**Risks and edge cases:** Main risks are schema ABI drift, missing or stale compatible coverage, incorrect min/max item constraints, wrong `#*-cells` values, resource names that diverge from driver expectations, conditionals that validate one SoC or chip variant while rejecting another valid one, and examples that no longer reflect real DTS users. Closure policy (`unevaluatedProperties: false`) is useful for catching misspellings but can also block legitimate extensions. For LED/backlight children, channel numbering and `reg`/`led-sources` constraints are common failure points; for IOMMU bindings, stream IDs and cell widths are especially sensitive; for IPMI bindings, host/BMC address and register-resource shape must match the driver.

**Test signals:** Run `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/leds/backlight/pwm-backlight.yaml` and `make dtbs_check` against DTS files using every compatible listed here. The 2 embedded example block(s) should compile through `dtc` and validate against referenced schemas. Useful negative tests remove each required property, add an unknown property under the declared closure policy, exercise compatible-specific conditionals, and verify the matching Linux driver still probes with the resources, child nodes, and phandle specifiers described by this binding.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/leds/common.yaml

**Purpose:** Defines the Linux Devicetree JSON-schema contract for `Common leds properties`. In this source tree it is a LED controller or common LED schema binding; it constrains board DTS/DTB nodes so kernel OF matching and subsystem code can discover the hardware resources consistently. This is declarative ABI documentation, not executable Ceph client code, but it is part of the imported kernel documentation corpus that protects hardware-description compatibility. The source description says: LED and flash LED devices provide the same basic functionality as current regulators, but extended with LED and flash LED specific features like blinking patterns, flash timeout, flash faults and external flash strobe mode. Many LED devices expose more than one current output that can be connected to one or more discrete LED component. Since the arrangement of connections can influence the way of the LED device initialization, the LED components have to be tightly coupled with the LED device binding. They are represented by child nodes of the parent LED device binding.

**Important APIs/types/functions:** The public surface is the schema itself: `$id` `http://devicetree.org/schemas/leds/common.yaml#`, `$schema` `http://devicetree.org/meta-schemas/core.yaml#`, `title`, `maintainers`, `properties`, `required`, composition keywords, closure rules, and `examples` consumed by `dt-schema`. Compatible strings are none declared. Required properties are none declared. Important top-level properties include `led-sources`, `function`, `color`, `function-enumerator`, `label`, `default-state`, `linux,default-trigger`, `led-pattern`, `led-max-microamp`, `max-brightness`, `default-brightness`, `panic-indicator`, `retain-state-shutdown`, `trigger-sources`, `active-high`, `active-low`, `inactive-high-impedance`, `flash-max-microamp`, `flash-max-timeout-us`. Patterned or child-node surfaces include none declared. Direct schema references are `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/phandle-array`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/string`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32-array`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32-matrix`. Maintainers: Jacek Anaszewski <jacek.anaszewski@gmail.com>, Pavel Machek <pavel@ucw.cz>. Top-level property policy is `additionalProperties: true`.

**Control flow:** Runtime control flow is supplied by Devicetree tooling and kernel drivers rather than by this YAML. `dt_binding_check` loads the core meta-schema, resolves `$ref` dependencies, applies constants/enums/cardinality/range checks, evaluates composition and conditional branches (`oneOf`, `allOf`, `if`, `then`), and validates the embedded examples. At boot, the compiled DTB node is matched by `compatible`; the relevant LED driver or common subsystem reads resource properties, phandles, child nodes, and cells before registering devices or provider relationships. Child/provider surfaces to watch here are none declared.

**State and persistence behavior:** The file owns no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data. Its persistence is ABI persistence: accepted DTS nodes encode hardware identity, register windows, interrupts, power/clock/reset topology, phandle cell widths, channel indexes, and child LED/IOMMU/IPMI layout for long-lived boards. Tightening `required`, changing compatible fallback order, altering cell counts, or changing closure policy can reject existing DTBs or make drivers parse resources differently.

**Dependencies and integration points:** Integrated with LED class devices, multicolor/flash/channel child nodes, GPIO/PWM/regulator resources, and trigger/function/color naming. Schema dependencies include `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/phandle-array`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/string`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32-array`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32-matrix`; examples may also include dt-bindings headers. Resource integration is through properties such as `max-brightness` for maximum logical brightness; `led-sources` for hardware output/channel selection; `function` for standard LED function name; `color` for standard LED color identifier; `function-enumerator` for disambiguates same-function LEDs; `linux,default-trigger` for default LED trigger binding; `flash-max-microamp` for flash LED current limit; `flash-max-timeout-us` for flash timeout limit; `led-max-microamp` for steady LED current limit. Driver integration is through `compatible`, while subsystem integration is through phandle arrays, provider cell counts, common schemas, and child-node schemas inherited or declared here.

**Risks and edge cases:** Main risks are schema ABI drift, missing or stale compatible coverage, incorrect min/max item constraints, wrong `#*-cells` values, resource names that diverge from driver expectations, conditionals that validate one SoC or chip variant while rejecting another valid one, and examples that no longer reflect real DTS users. Closure policy (`additionalProperties: true`) is useful for catching misspellings but can also block legitimate extensions. For LED/backlight children, channel numbering and `reg`/`led-sources` constraints are common failure points; for IOMMU bindings, stream IDs and cell widths are especially sensitive; for IPMI bindings, host/BMC address and register-resource shape must match the driver.

**Test signals:** Run `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/leds/common.yaml` and `make dtbs_check` against DTS files using every compatible listed here. The 3 embedded example block(s) should compile through `dtc` and validate against referenced schemas. Useful negative tests remove each required property, add an unknown property under the declared closure policy, exercise compatible-specific conditionals, and verify the matching Linux driver still probes with the resources, child nodes, and phandle specifiers described by this binding.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/leds/leds-bcm6328.yaml

**Purpose:** Defines the Linux Devicetree JSON-schema contract for `LEDs connected to Broadcom BCM6328 controller`. In this source tree it is a LED controller or common LED schema binding; it constrains board DTS/DTB nodes so kernel OF matching and subsystem code can discover the hardware resources consistently. This is declarative ABI documentation, not executable Ceph client code, but it is part of the imported kernel documentation corpus that protects hardware-description compatibility. The source description says: This controller is present on BCM6318, BCM6328, BCM6362 and BCM63268. In these SoCs it's possible to control LEDs both as GPIOs or by hardware. However, on some devices there are Serial LEDs (LEDs connected to a 74x164 controller), which can either be controlled by software (exporting the 74x164 as spi-gpio. See Documentation/devicetree/bindings/gpio/fairchild,74hc595.yaml), or by hardware using this driver. Some of these Serial LEDs are hardware controlled (e.g. ethernet LEDs) and exporting the 74x164 as spi-gpio prevents those LEDs to be hardware controlled, so the only chance to keep them working is by using this driver. BCM6328 LED controller has a HWDIS register, which controls whether a LED should be controlled by a hardware signal instead of the MODE register value, with 0 meaning hardware control enabled and 1 hardware control disabled. This is usually 1:1 for hardware to LED signals, but through the activity/link registers you have some limited control over rerouting the LEDs (as explained later in brcm,link-signal-sources). Even if a LED is hardware controlled you are still able to make it blink or light it up if it isn't, but you can't turn it off if the hardware decides to light it up. For this reason, hardware controlled LEDs aren't registered as LED class devices. Each LED is represented as a sub-node of the brcm,bcm6328-leds device.

**Important APIs/types/functions:** The public surface is the schema itself: `$id` `http://devicetree.org/schemas/leds/leds-bcm6328.yaml#`, `$schema` `http://devicetree.org/meta-schemas/core.yaml#`, `title`, `maintainers`, `properties`, `required`, composition keywords, closure rules, and `examples` consumed by `dt-schema`. Compatible strings are `brcm,bcm6328-leds`. Required properties are `reg`, `#address-cells`, `#size-cells`. Important top-level properties include `compatible`, `reg`, `brcm,serial-leds`, `brcm,serial-mux`, `brcm,serial-clk-low`, `brcm,serial-dat-low`, `brcm,serial-shift-inv`, `#address-cells`, `#size-cells`. Patterned or child-node surfaces include `@[a-f0-9]+$`. Direct schema references are `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32-array`, `common.yaml#`. Maintainers: Álvaro Fernández Rojas <noltari@gmail.com>. Top-level property policy is `additionalProperties: false`.

**Control flow:** Runtime control flow is supplied by Devicetree tooling and kernel drivers rather than by this YAML. `dt_binding_check` loads the core meta-schema, resolves `$ref` dependencies, applies constants/enums/cardinality/range checks, evaluates composition and conditional branches (none declared), and validates the embedded examples. At boot, the compiled DTB node is matched by `compatible`; the relevant LED driver or common subsystem reads resource properties, phandles, child nodes, and cells before registering devices or provider relationships. Child/provider surfaces to watch here are `@[a-f0-9]+$`.

**State and persistence behavior:** The file owns no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data. Its persistence is ABI persistence: accepted DTS nodes encode hardware identity, register windows, interrupts, power/clock/reset topology, phandle cell widths, channel indexes, and child LED/IOMMU/IPMI layout for long-lived boards. Tightening `required`, changing compatible fallback order, altering cell counts, or changing closure policy can reject existing DTBs or make drivers parse resources differently.

**Dependencies and integration points:** Integrated with LED class devices, multicolor/flash/channel child nodes, GPIO/PWM/regulator resources, and trigger/function/color naming. Resource integration is through properties such as `compatible` for driver match key and hardware ABI identifier; `reg` for MMIO, I2C/SPI address, or child register address; `#address-cells` for child address cell layout; `#size-cells` for child size cell layout. Driver integration is through `compatible`, while subsystem integration is through phandle arrays, provider cell counts, common schemas, and child-node schemas inherited or declared here.

**Risks and edge cases:** Main risks are schema ABI drift, missing or stale compatible coverage, incorrect min/max item constraints, wrong `#*-cells` values, resource names that diverge from driver expectations, conditionals that validate one SoC or chip variant while rejecting another valid one, and examples that no longer reflect real DTS users. Closure policy (`additionalProperties: false`) is useful for catching misspellings but can also block legitimate extensions. For LED/backlight children, channel numbering and `reg`/`led-sources` constraints are common failure points; for IOMMU bindings, stream IDs and cell widths are especially sensitive; for IPMI bindings, host/BMC address and register-resource shape must match the driver.

**Purpose:** Defines the Linux Devicetree JSON-schema contract for `Flash LED device inside Qualcomm Technologies, Inc. PMICs`. In this source tree it is a LED controller or common LED schema binding; it constrains board DTS/DTB nodes so kernel OF matching and subsystem code can discover the hardware resources consistently. This is declarative ABI documentation, not executable Ceph client code, but it is part of the imported kernel documentation corpus that protects hardware-description compatibility. The source description says: Flash LED controller is present inside some Qualcomm Technologies, Inc. PMICs. The flash LED module can have different number of LED channels supported e.g. 3 or 4. There are some different registers between them but they can both support maximum current up to 1.5 A per channel and they can also support ganging 2 channels together to supply maximum current up to 2 A. The current will be split symmetrically on each channel and they will be enabled and disabled at the same time.

**Important APIs/types/functions:** The public surface is the schema itself: `$id` `http://devicetree.org/schemas/leds/qcom,spmi-flash-led.yaml#`, `$schema` `http://devicetree.org/meta-schemas/core.yaml#`, `title`, `maintainers`, `properties`, `required`, composition keywords, closure rules, and `examples` consumed by `dt-schema`. Compatible strings are `qcom,pm6150l-flash-led`, `qcom,pm660l-flash-led`, `qcom,pm7550-flash-led`, `qcom,pm8150c-flash-led`, `qcom,pm8150l-flash-led`, `qcom,pm8350c-flash-led`, `qcom,pm8550-flash-led`, `qcom,pmh0101-flash-led`, `qcom,pmi8998-flash-led`, `qcom,spmi-flash-led`. Required properties are `compatible`, `reg`. Important top-level properties include `compatible`, `reg`. Patterned or child-node surfaces include `^led-[0-3]$`. Direct schema references are `common.yaml#`. Maintainers: Fenglin Wu <quic_fenglinw@quicinc.com>. Top-level property policy is `additionalProperties: false`.

**Control flow:** Runtime control flow is supplied by Devicetree tooling and kernel drivers rather than by this YAML. `dt_binding_check` loads the core meta-schema, resolves `$ref` dependencies, applies constants/enums/cardinality/range checks, evaluates composition and conditional branches (`allOf`, `anyOf`), and validates the embedded examples. At boot, the compiled DTB node is matched by `compatible`; the relevant LED driver or common subsystem reads resource properties, phandles, child nodes, and cells before registering devices or provider relationships. Child/provider surfaces to watch here are `^led-[0-3]$`.

**State and persistence behavior:** The file owns no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data. Its persistence is ABI persistence: accepted DTS nodes encode hardware identity, register windows, interrupts, power/clock/reset topology, phandle cell widths, channel indexes, and child LED/IOMMU/IPMI layout for long-lived boards. Tightening `required`, changing compatible fallback order, altering cell counts, or changing closure policy can reject existing DTBs or make drivers parse resources differently.

**Dependencies and integration points:** Integrated with LED class devices, multicolor/flash/channel child nodes, GPIO/PWM/regulator resources, and trigger/function/color naming. Schema dependencies include `common.yaml#`; examples may also include dt-bindings headers. Resource integration is through properties such as `compatible` for driver match key and hardware ABI identifier; `reg` for MMIO, I2C/SPI address, or child register address. Driver integration is through `compatible`, while subsystem integration is through phandle arrays, provider cell counts, common schemas, and child-node schemas inherited or declared here.

**Risks and edge cases:** Main risks are schema ABI drift, missing or stale compatible coverage, incorrect min/max item constraints, wrong `#*-cells` values, resource names that diverge from driver expectations, conditionals that validate one SoC or chip variant while rejecting another valid one, and examples that no longer reflect real DTS users. Closure policy (`additionalProperties: false`) is useful for catching misspellings but can also block legitimate extensions. For LED/backlight children, channel numbering and `reg`/`led-sources` constraints are common failure points; for IOMMU bindings, stream IDs and cell widths are especially sensitive; for IPMI bindings, host/BMC address and register-resource shape must match the driver.

**Test signals:** Run `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/leds/qcom,spmi-flash-led.yaml` and `make dtbs_check` against DTS files using every compatible listed here. The 1 embedded example block(s) should compile through `dtc` and validate against referenced schemas. Useful negative tests remove each required property, add an unknown property under the declared closure policy, exercise compatible-specific conditionals, and verify the matching Linux driver still probes with the resources, child nodes, and phandle specifiers described by this binding.

Its persistence is ABI persistence: accepted DTS nodes encode hardware identity, register windows, interrupts, power/clock/reset topology, phandle cell widths, channel indexes, and child LED/IOMMU/IPMI layout for long-lived boards. Tightening `required`, changing compatible fallback order, altering cell counts, or changing closure policy can reject existing DTBs or make drivers parse resources differently.

**Dependencies and integration points:** Integrated with LED class devices, multicolor/flash/channel child nodes, GPIO/PWM/regulator resources, and trigger/function/color naming. Schema dependencies include `common.yaml#`; examples may also include dt-bindings headers. Resource integration is through properties such as `compatible` for driver match key and hardware ABI identifier; `reg` for MMIO, I2C/SPI address, or child register address; `#address-cells` for child address cell layout; `#size-cells` for child size cell layout; `enable-gpios` for enable GPIO control; `vled-supply` for LED/backlight supply regulator. Driver integration is through `compatible`, while subsystem integration is through phandle arrays, provider cell counts, common schemas, and child-node schemas inherited or declared here.

**Risks and edge cases:** Main risks are schema ABI drift, missing or stale compatible coverage, incorrect min/max item constraints, wrong `#*-cells` values, resource names that diverge from driver expectations, conditionals that validate one SoC or chip variant while rejecting another valid one, and examples that no longer reflect real DTS users. Closure policy (`additionalProperties: false`) is useful for catching misspellings but can also block legitimate extensions. For LED/backlight children, channel numbering and `reg`/`led-sources` constraints are common failure points; for IOMMU bindings, stream IDs and cell widths are especially sensitive; for IPMI bindings, host/BMC address and register-resource shape must match the driver.

**Test signals:** Run `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/leds/ti,lp8860.yaml` and `make dtbs_check` against DTS files using every compatible listed here. The 1 embedded example block(s) should compile through `dtc` and validate against referenced schemas. Useful negative tests remove each required property, add an unknown property under the declared closure policy, exercise compatible-specific conditionals, and verify the matching Linux driver still probes with the resources, child nodes, and phandle specifiers described by this binding.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/leds/ti,tca6507.yaml

**Purpose:** Defines the Linux Devicetree JSON-schema contract for `TCA6507 LED and GPIO controller`. In this source tree it is a LED controller or common LED schema binding; it constrains board DTS/DTB nodes so kernel OF matching and subsystem code can discover the hardware resources consistently. This is declarative ABI documentation, not executable Ceph client code, but it is part of the imported kernel documentation corpus that protects hardware-description compatibility. The source description says: The TCA6507 is a programmable LED controller connected via I2C that can drive 7 separate lines either by holding them low, or by pulsing them with modulated width.
